If I start eating more, especially when my wife’s putting butter on things or gives me one of those full plates, I get this worry that it’ll all creep back on and I won’t be able to keep a handle on it.\n\nShe means well, of course—she’s always been that way with food, very generous—but when I see it, I think, no, I’ve worked hard to keep control, and I’m afraid of losing that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yes, I suppose it does, in a way. Eating less makes me feel like I’m keeping a handle on things. It’s not that I sit around thinking about it all day—well, not unless it’s mealtime.

Like when my wife puts butter on things, or gives me a plate piled up the way she always has—she was brought up that way, you know, “a good meal keeps you going,” her mother used to say—then I get nervous. I think if I let that go, I’ll start putting weight back on and lose the control I’ve worked hard to keep.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, my wife, really, she’s the one who wanted me to come in. She says I’m not eating enough. I don’t know… most days I just have coffee and a piece of toast in the morning, and later maybe some soup or vegetables. I’ve never been one for big meals, even when I was younger—my father used to say dinner should “stick to your ribs,” but that always seemed like too much to me. At my age, big meals just feel unnecessary, you know?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose I have, at least that’s what my doctor keeps saying—the number on the scale has gotten too low. But I don’t know, being a bit lighter doesn’t feel like such a terrible thing to me. It makes it easier to move around the garden, and I can keep up with my walks better. I’ve always liked being out there with the tomatoes and the beans, you know, bending down and not feeling weighed down.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about gaining weight if you eat more than you currently do?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, a bit. When my wife puts butter on the toast or piles the plate up like she’s feeding a farmhand, I get nervous. Not angry, exactly, just uneasy, like I’ll start putting weight back on and lose the control I’ve kept.\n\nShe means well, of course. She’s always been a good cook—used to make these big Sunday dinners when the children were around—but now it just feels like too much on the plate. So yes, I do worry about it some.\n\nDoctor: How often do you think about gaining weight during the day?\n\nPatient: Not all day, no… at least I don’t think so. It’s more when food is right in front of me. Like if my wife puts butter on the toast or gives me a plate that looks too full, then I start thinking, “Oh, here we go, I’ll put the weight back on.” It makes me nervous, like I’ll lose the control I’ve been trying to keep.\n\nFunny, she’s always believed a proper meal fixes everything—her mother was the same way, big plates, gravy on everything—but for me it just sort of sits in my mind when she serves it that way.\n\nDoctor: Is the worry about gaining weight something that you think about even when you're not eating or thinking about food?\n\nPatient: Not so much when I’m out in the garden or reading the paper, no. It’s mostly when food’s there in front of me—like if my wife tries to put butter on things, or serves me one of those full plates. Then I get nervous I’ll start putting weight back on and lose the control I’ve been keeping.\n\nShe’s always been generous with food, bless her… never could make a small portion of anything. But yes, it’s more around meals than just popping into my head all day.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat more if you weren't worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I might, yes… if that nervous feeling wasn’t there. It’s usually when my wife’s putting butter on something or gives me a plate that looks too full, then I start thinking I’ll put weight back on and lose the control I’ve managed to keep.\n\nShe’s always been one for proper meals, you know—never skimps on anything. I suppose if I didn’t get that worry in my head, I’d probably manage a bit more.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would lose control if you started eating more?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s the feeling, I suppose. If I start eating more, especially when my wife’s putting butter on things or gives me one of those full plates, I get this worry that it’ll all creep back on and I won’t be able to keep a handle on it.\n\nShe means well, of course—she’s always been that way with food, very generous—but when I see it, I think, no, I’ve worked hard to keep control, and I’m afraid of losing that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like eating less helps you feel more in control of your life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose it does, in a way. Eating less makes me feel like I’m keeping a handle on things. It’s not that I sit around thinking about it all day—well, not unless it’s mealtime.\n\nLike when my wife puts butter on things, or gives me a plate piled up the way she always has—she was brought up that way, you know, “a good meal keeps you going,” her mother used to say—then I get nervous. I think if I let that go, I’ll start putting weight back on and lose the control I’ve worked hard to keep.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits are a way of maintaining control over other aspects of your life?\n\nPatient: I suppose it might be, yes… though I don’t know that I think of it in such a big way. It’s more at the table, really. If my wife puts butter on something, or gives me a plate that looks too full, I get this nervous feeling that I’ll start putting weight back on and lose the control I’ve been trying to keep.\n\nShe’s always done meals properly—big portions, bit of butter, all that. Her mother was the same, actually. But for me, when I see it, I think, no, I can’t let that slip. So yes, maybe it is about control, in my own way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your eating habits are the main way you maintain control in your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it’s the main way… I mean, at my age there aren’t so many things I’m in charge of the way I used to be. I used to have my routines, work and all that, and now it’s mostly the house, appointments, the garden when the weather’s decent.\n\nBut with food, yes, that’s where I feel it most. If my wife puts butter on something, or gives me one of those full plates—she’s only trying to look after me—I get nervous. I think, if I start eating like that, the weight will come back on, and then I’ve lost the control I’ve worked so hard to keep. So… maybe it is one of the main ways, yes.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. I’ve always liked being out there with the tomatoes and the beans, you know, bending down and not feeling weighed down.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about gaining weight if you eat more than you currently do?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, a bit. When my wife puts butter on the toast or piles the plate up like she’s feeding a farmhand, I get nervous. Not angry, exactly, just uneasy, like I’ll start putting weight back on and lose the control I’ve kept.\n\nShe means well, of course. She’s always been a good cook—used to make these big Sunday dinners when the children were around—but now it just feels like too much on the plate. It’s mostly when food’s there in front of me—like if my wife tries to put butter on things, or serves me one of those full plates. But yes, it’s more around meals than just popping into my head all day.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat more if you weren't worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I might, yes… if that nervous feeling wasn’t there. It’s usually when my wife’s putting butter on something or gives me a plate that looks too full, then I start thinking I’ll put weight back on and lose the control I’ve managed to keep.\n\nShe’s always been one for proper meals, you know—never skimps on anything. I suppose if I didn’t get that worry in my head, I’d probably manage a bit more.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would lose control if you started eating more?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s the feeling, I suppose. If I start eating more, especially when my wife’s putting butter on things or gives me one of those full plates, I get this worry that it’ll all creep back on and I won’t be able to keep a handle on it.\n\nShe means well, of course—she’s always been that way with food, very generous—but when I see it, I think, no, I’ve worked hard to keep control, and I’m afraid of losing that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like eating less helps you feel more in control of your life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose it does, in a way. Eating less makes me feel like I’m keeping a handle on things. It’s not that I sit around thinking about it all day—well, not unless it’s mealtime.\n\nLike when my wife puts butter on things, or gives me a plate piled up the way she always has—she was brought up that way, you know, “a good meal keeps you going,” her mother used to say—then I get nervous. I think if I let that go, I’ll start putting weight back on and lose the control I’ve worked hard to keep.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits are a way of maintaining control over other aspects of your life?\n\nPatient: I suppose it might be, yes… though I don’t know that I think of it in such a big way. It’s more at the table, really. If my wife puts butter on something, or gives me a plate that looks too full, I get this nervous feeling that I’ll start putting weight back on and lose the control I’ve been trying to keep.\n\nShe’s always done meals properly—big portions, bit of butter, all that. Her mother was the same, actually. But for me, when I see it, I think, no, I can’t let that slip. So yes, maybe it is about control, in my own way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your eating habits are the main way you maintain control in your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it’s the main way… I mean, at my age there aren’t so many things I’m in charge of the way I used to be. I used to have my routines, work and all that, and now it’s mostly the house, appointments, the garden when the weather’s decent.\n\nBut with food, yes, that’s where I feel it most. If my wife puts butter on something, or gives me one of those full plates—she’s only trying to look after me—I get nervous. I think, if I start eating like that, the weight will come back on, and then I’ve lost the control I’ve worked so hard to keep. So… maybe it is one of the main ways, yes.\n\nDoctor: Is your decision to eat less mostly because you are afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly, I think. It’s the weight coming back that worries me.\n\nLike if my wife puts butter on the potatoes or gives me a plate that looks too full, I get all on edge. She means well, she’s always cooked that way—proper meals, you know—but I look at it and think, if I start letting that in again, I’ll put weight back on and lose the bit of control I’ve managed to keep. It’s more when food is right in front of me. It’s mostly when food’s there in front of me—like if my wife tries to put butter on things, or serves me one of those full plates. Then I get nervous I’ll start putting weight back on and lose the control I’ve been keeping.\n\nShe’s always been generous with food, bless her… never could make a small portion of anything. But yes, it’s more around meals than just popping into my head all day.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat more if you weren't worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I might, yes… if that nervous feeling wasn’t there. I don’t know about “look good,” exactly. My wife would probably say I’d look better with a bit more on me—she’s said things like that before, in her way.

But when I look in the mirror, if my stomach seems even a little rounded, I feel… sloppy, I suppose. Disappointed in myself. And then silly things matter, like if my belt feels a notch looser, I think, well, I’ve done right today. It’s daft, I know. I used to have a brown leather belt I wore for years, had to punch an extra hole in it once—anyway… no, I don’t really think I’d look good gaining weight, not in the way I see myself.

I’ve always liked being out there with the tomatoes and the beans, you know, bending down and not feeling weighed down.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about gaining weight if you eat more than you currently do?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, a bit. It’s mostly when food’s there in front of me—like if my wife tries to put butter on things, or serves me one of those full plates. Then I get nervous I’ll start putting weight back on and lose the control I’ve been keeping.\n\nShe’s always been generous with food, bless her… never could make a small portion of anything. But yes, it’s more around meals than just popping into my head all day.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat more if you weren't worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I might, yes… if that nervous feeling wasn’t there. It’s usually when my wife’s putting butter on something or gives me a plate that looks too full, then I start thinking I’ll put weight back on and lose the control I’ve managed to keep.\n\nShe’s always been one for proper meals, you know—never skimps on anything. I suppose if I didn’t get that worry in my head, I’d probably manage a bit more.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would lose control if you started eating more?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s the feeling, I suppose. If I start eating more, especially when my wife’s putting butter on things or gives me one of those full plates, I get this worry that it’ll all creep back on and I won’t be able to keep a handle on it.\n\nShe means well, of course—she’s always been that way with food, very generous—but when I see it, I think, no, I’ve worked hard to keep control, and I’m afraid of losing that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like eating less helps you feel more in control of your life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose it does, in a way. Eating less makes me feel like I’m keeping a handle on things. It’s not that I sit around thinking about it all day—well, not unless it’s mealtime.\n\nLike when my wife puts butter on things, or gives me a plate piled up the way she always has—she was brought up that way, you know, “a good meal keeps you going,” her mother used to say—then I get nervous. I think if I let that go, I’ll start putting weight back on and lose the control I’ve worked hard to keep.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits are a way of maintaining control over other aspects of your life?\n\nPatient: I suppose it might be, yes… though I don’t know that I think of it in such a big way. It’s more at the table, really. If my wife puts butter on something, or gives me a plate that looks too full, I get this nervous feeling that I’ll start putting weight back on and lose the control I’ve been trying to keep.\n\nShe’s always done meals properly—big portions, bit of butter, all that. If my wife puts butter on something, or gives me one of those full plates—she’s only trying to look after me—I get nervous. I think, if I start eating like that, the weight will come back on, and then I’ve lost the control I’ve worked so hard to keep. So… maybe it is one of the main ways, yes.\n\nDoctor: Is your decision to eat less mostly because you are afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly, I think. It’s the weight coming back that worries me.\n\nLike if my wife puts butter on the potatoes or gives me a plate that looks too full, I get all on edge. She means well, she’s always cooked that way—proper meals, you know—but I look at it and think, if I start letting that in again, I’ll put weight back on and lose the bit of control I’ve managed to keep. So yes, that fear is a big part of why I eat less.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of gaining weight the main reason you restrict your food intake?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it probably is. I don’t like saying it that plainly, but when my wife puts butter on things, or she gives me a plate that looks too full, I get this tight, nervous feeling.\n\nIt reminds me of how she’s always done meals—proper portions, like people used to. Disappointed in myself. And then silly things matter, like if my belt feels a notch looser, I think, well, I’ve done right today. It’s daft, I know. I suppose if I didn’t get that worry in my head, I’d probably manage a bit more.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would lose control if you started eating more?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s the feeling, I suppose. If I start eating more, especially when my wife’s putting butter on things or gives me one of those full plates, I get this worry that it’ll all creep back on and I won’t be able to keep a handle on it.\n\nShe means well, of course—she’s always been that way with food, very generous—but when I see it, I think, no, I’ve worked hard to keep control, and I’m afraid of losing that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like eating less helps you feel more in control of your life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose it does, in a way. Eating less makes me feel like I’m keeping a handle on things. It’s not that I sit around thinking about it all day—well, not unless it’s mealtime.\n\nLike when my wife puts butter on things, or gives me a plate piled up the way she always has—she was brought up that way, you know, “a good meal keeps you going,” her mother used to say—then I get nervous. Not the only reason, perhaps, but it’s a big part of it, if I’m being honest.

When my wife puts butter on things—she does that, says it’ll “do me good”—or gives me a full plate, I get… unsettled. Nervous, really. Like if I let that start, I’ll just put weight back on and lose the bit of control I’ve worked quite hard to keep.

It sounds silly when I say it. She used to make lovely roast potatoes, years ago, proper crisp ones… everyone would have seconds. But now I see a plate piled up and I’m already thinking how to leave some of it without making a row.

If my wife puts butter on something, or gives me a plate that looks too full, I get this nervous feeling that I’ll start putting weight back on and lose the control I’ve been trying to keep.\n\nShe’s always done meals properly—big portions, bit of butter, all that. Her mother was the same, actually. But for me, when I see it, I think, no, I can’t let that slip. So yes, maybe it is about control, in my own way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your eating habits are the main way you maintain control in your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it’s the main way… I mean, at my age there aren’t so many things I’m in charge of the way I used to be. I used to have my routines, work and all that, and now it’s mostly the house, appointments, the garden when the weather’s decent.\n\nBut with food, yes, that’s where I feel it most. If my wife puts butter on something, or gives me one of those full plates—she’s only trying to look after me—I get nervous. I think, if I start eating like that, the weight will come back on, and then I’ve lost the control I’ve worked so hard to keep. So… maybe it is one of the main ways, yes.\n\nDoctor: Is your decision to eat less mostly because you are afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly, I think. It’s the weight coming back that worries me.\n\nLike if my wife puts butter on the potatoes or gives me a plate that looks too full, I get all on edge. She means well, she’s always cooked that way—proper meals, you know—but I look at it and think, if I start letting that in again, I’ll put weight back on and lose the bit of control I’ve managed to keep. So yes, that fear is a big part of why I eat less.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of gaining weight the main reason you restrict your food intake?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it probably is. I don’t like saying it that plainly, but when my wife puts butter on things, or she gives me a plate that looks too full, I get this tight, nervous feeling.\n\nIt reminds me of how she’s always done meals—proper portions, like people used to. My mother was a bit like that too, never liked waste. But I look at it now and think, if I start eating all that, the weight will come back on, and then I’ve lost the control I’ve tried to keep. So yes, the fear of gaining weight is a big part of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would look good if you gained a bit of weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t know about “look good,” exactly. My wife would probably say I’d look better with a bit more on me—she’s said things like that before, in her way.\n\nBut when I look in the mirror, if my stomach seems even a little rounded, I feel… sloppy, I suppose. Disappointed in myself. And then silly things matter, like if my belt feels a notch looser, I think, well, I’ve done right today. It’s daft, I know. I used to have a brown leather belt I wore for years, had to punch an extra hole in it once—anyway… no, I don’t really think I’d look good gaining weight, not in the way I see myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look too thin now?\n\nPatient: Too thin? I suppose it’s a bit of both, though I wouldn’t say I’m trying to look like anything in particular. At my age, you’re not standing in front of the mirror admiring yourself, are you?

But… if my belt feels a notch looser, I do feel like I’ve done right by myself somehow. Like I’ve kept things in order. And then if I catch myself in the mirror and my stomach looks even a little rounded, I feel… sloppy, I suppose. Disappointed with myself.

Funny, I still have an old belt from years ago, good leather one, lasted better than most things we bought back then. They don’t make them like that now. But yes, I do notice where it sits. More than I probably should.

I’ve always liked being out there with the tomatoes and the beans, you know, bending down and not feeling weighed down.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about gaining weight if you eat more than you currently do?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, a bit. When my wife puts butter on the toast or piles the plate up like she’s feeding a farmhand, I get nervous. Not angry, exactly, just uneasy, like I’ll start putting weight back on and lose the control I’ve kept.\n\nShe means well, of course. She’s always been a good cook—used to make these big Sunday dinners when the children were around—but now it just feels like too much on the plate. So yes, I do worry about it some.\n\nDoctor: How often do you think about gaining weight during the day?\n\nPatient: Not all day, no… at least I don’t think so. It’s more when food is right in front of me. Like if my wife puts butter on the toast or gives me a plate that looks too full, then I start thinking, “Oh, here we go, I’ll put the weight back on.” It makes me nervous, like I’ll lose the control I’ve been trying to keep.\n\nFunny, she’s always believed a proper meal fixes everything—her mother was the same way, big plates, gravy on everything—but for me it just sort of sits in my mind when she serves it that way.\n\nDoctor: Is the worry about gaining weight something that you think about even when you're not eating or thinking about food?\n\nPatient: Not so much when I’m out in the garden or reading the paper, no. It’s mostly when food’s there in front of me—like if my wife tries to put butter on things, or serves me one of those full plates. Then I get nervous I’ll start putting weight back on and lose the control I’ve been keeping.\n\nShe’s always been generous with food, bless her… never could make a small portion of anything. But yes, it’s more around meals than just popping into my head all day.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat more if you weren't worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I might, yes… if that nervous feeling wasn’t there. It’s usually when my wife’s putting butter on something or gives me a plate that looks too full, then I start thinking I’ll put weight back on and lose the control I’ve managed to keep.\n\nShe’s always been one for proper meals, you know—never skimps on anything. I suppose if I didn’t get that worry in my head, I’d probably manage a bit more.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would lose control if you started eating more?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s the feeling, I suppose. If I start eating more, especially when my wife’s putting butter on things or gives me one of those full plates, I get this worry that it’ll all creep back on and I won’t be able to keep a handle on it.\n\nShe means well, of course—she’s always been that way with food, very generous—but when I see it, I think, no, I’ve worked hard to keep control, and I’m afraid of losing that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like eating less helps you feel more in control of your life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose it does, in a way. Eating less makes me feel like I’m keeping a handle on things. It’s not that I sit around thinking about it all day—well, not unless it’s mealtime.\n\nLike when my wife puts butter on things, or gives me a plate piled up the way she always has—she was brought up that way, you know, “a good meal keeps you going,” her mother used to say—then I get nervous. I think if I let that go, I’ll start putting weight back on and lose the control I’ve worked hard to keep.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits are a way of maintaining control over other aspects of your life?\n\nPatient: I suppose it might be, yes… though I don’t know that I think of it in such a big way. It’s more at the table, really. If my wife puts butter on something, or gives me a plate that looks too full, I get this nervous feeling that I’ll start putting weight back on and lose the control I’ve been trying to keep.\n\nShe’s always done meals properly—big portions, bit of butter, all that. Her mother was the same, actually. But for me, when I see it, I think, no, I can’t let that slip. So yes, maybe it is about control, in my own way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your eating habits are the main way you maintain control in your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it’s the main way… I mean, at my age there aren’t so many things I’m in charge of the way I used to be. I used to have my routines, work and all that, and now it’s mostly the house, appointments, the garden when the weather’s decent.\n\nBut with food, yes, that’s where I feel it most. If my wife puts butter on something, or gives me one of those full plates—she’s only trying to look after me—I get nervous. I think, if I start eating like that, the weight will come back on, and then I’ve lost the control I’ve worked so hard to keep. So… maybe it is one of the main ways, yes.\n\nDoctor: Is your decision to eat less mostly because you are afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly, I think. It’s the weight coming back that worries me.\n\nLike if my wife puts butter on the potatoes or gives me a plate that looks too full, I get all on edge. She means well, she’s always cooked that way—proper meals, you know—but I look at it and think, if I start letting that in again, I’ll put weight back on and lose the bit of control I’ve managed to keep. So yes, that fear is a big part of why I eat less.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of gaining weight the main reason you restrict your food intake?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it probably is. I don’t like saying it that plainly, but when my wife puts butter on things, or she gives me a plate that looks too full, I get this tight, nervous feeling.\n\nIt reminds me of how she’s always done meals—proper portions, like people used to. My mother was a bit like that too, never liked waste. But I look at it now and think, if I start eating all that, the weight will come back on, and then I’ve lost the control I’ve tried to keep. So yes, the fear of gaining weight is a big part of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would look good if you gained a bit of weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t know about “look good,” exactly. My wife would probably say I’d look better with a bit more on me—she’s said things like that before, in her way.\n\nBut when I look in the mirror, if my stomach seems even a little rounded, I feel… sloppy, I suppose. Disappointed in myself. And then silly things matter, like if my belt feels a notch looser, I think, well, I’ve done right today. It’s daft, I know. I used to have a brown leather belt I wore for years, had to punch an extra hole in it once—anyway… no, I don’t really think I’d look good gaining weight, not in the way I see myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look too thin now?\n\nPatient: Too thin? I don’t really see it that way, no. I mean, I know people keep making a fuss, my wife especially, but at my age you don’t eat like you did when you were thirty, do you?\n\nI’m still getting about, doing my chores and going on my little tours when I can. I did one last week—well, not a proper tour, just out and about—but I managed fine. So I suppose I don’t quite understand why everyone’s so worried about my weight.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is currently at a healthy level for your body?\n\nPatient: I think it’s healthy enough, yes. I mean, I don’t really see why everyone’s making such a fuss. Older people don’t eat the way they did when they were younger, do they?\n\nI still manage my chores, and I still get out on my little tours when I can—well, not grand trips or anything, just my usual rounds. So from where I’m standing, if I’m still doing all that, it can’t be as bad as people make out.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the only reason you eat less than you probably should?\n\nPatient: Not the only reason, perhaps, but it’s a big part of it, if I’m being honest.\n\nWhen my wife puts butter on things—she does that, says it’ll “do me good”—or gives me a full plate, I get… unsettled. Nervous, really. Like if I let that start, I’ll just put weight back on and lose the bit of control I’ve worked quite hard to keep.\n\nIt sounds silly when I say it. She used to make lovely roast potatoes, years ago, proper crisp ones… everyone would have seconds. But now I see a plate piled up and I’m already thinking how to leave some of it without making a row.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the way it should be because of how you want to look, or because of how you want to feel?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s a bit of both, though I wouldn’t say I’m trying to look like anything in particular. At my age, you’re not standing in front of the mirror admiring yourself, are you?\n\nBut… if my belt feels a notch looser, I do feel like I’ve done right by myself somehow. Like I’ve kept things in order. And then if I catch myself in the mirror and my stomach looks even a little rounded, I feel… sloppy, I suppose. Disappointed with myself.\n\nFunny, I still have an old belt from years ago, good leather one, lasted better than most things we bought back then. They don’t make them like that now. But yes, I do notice where it sits. More than I probably should.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Like I’ve kept things in order. And then if I catch myself in the mirror and my stomach looks even a little rounded, I feel… sloppy, I suppose. Disappointed with myself.\n\nFunny, I still have an old belt from years ago, good leather one, lasted better than most things we bought back then. They don’t make them like that now. But yes, I do notice where it sits. More than I probably should.\n\n[Last Question]\nIs your fear of gaining weight the main reason you eat less than you probably should?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 78 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Fear_of_Weight_Gain\nmanifestation: \"When my wife tries to put butter on things or serves me a full plate, I get nervous that I'll start putting weight back on and lose the control I've worked to keep.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I’d say it’s the main one, really. I don’t like admitting that, but when my wife puts butter on things, or gives me one of those plates filled right to the edges, I get nervous.

It’s not the butter itself, you know—well, I suppose it is—but it’s more the thought that if I start eating like that again I’ll put weight back on, and then I’ll lose the bit of control I’ve managed to keep. My wife means well, of course. She’s always been one for making sure people are fed properly. But yes, that fear is a big part of why I hold back.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, my wife, really, she’s the one who wanted me to come in. She says I’m not eating enough. I don’t know… most days I just have coffee and a piece of toast in the morning, and later maybe some soup or vegetables. I’ve never been one for big meals, even when I was younger—my father used to say dinner should “stick to your ribs,” but that always seemed like too much to me. At my age, big meals just feel unnecessary, you know?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose I have, at least that’s what my doctor keeps saying—the number on the scale has gotten too low. But I don’t know, being a bit lighter doesn’t feel like such a terrible thing to me. It makes it easier to move around the garden, and I can keep up with my walks better. I’ve always liked being out there with the tomatoes and the beans, you know, bending down and not feeling weighed down.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about gaining weight if you eat more than you currently do?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, a bit. When my wife puts butter on the toast or piles the plate up like she’s feeding a farmhand, I get nervous. Not angry, exactly, just uneasy, like I’ll start putting weight back on and lose the control I’ve kept.\n\nShe means well, of course. She’s always been a good cook—used to make these big Sunday dinners when the children were around—but now it just feels like too much on the plate. So yes, I do worry about it some.\n\nDoctor: How often do you think about gaining weight during the day?\n\nPatient: Not all day, no… at least I don’t think so. It’s more when food is right in front of me. Like if my wife puts butter on the toast or gives me a plate that looks too full, then I start thinking, “Oh, here we go, I’ll put the weight back on.” It makes me nervous, like I’ll lose the control I’ve been trying to keep.\n\nFunny, she’s always believed a proper meal fixes everything—her mother was the same way, big plates, gravy on everything—but for me it just sort of sits in my mind when she serves it that way.\n\nDoctor: Is the worry about gaining weight something that you think about even when you're not eating or thinking about food?\n\nPatient: Not so much when I’m out in the garden or reading the paper, no. It’s mostly when food’s there in front of me—like if my wife tries to put butter on things, or serves me one of those full plates. Then I get nervous I’ll start putting weight back on and lose the control I’ve been keeping.\n\nShe’s always been generous with food, bless her… never could make a small portion of anything. But yes, it’s more around meals than just popping into my head all day.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat more if you weren't worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I might, yes… if that nervous feeling wasn’t there. It’s usually when my wife’s putting butter on something or gives me a plate that looks too full, then I start thinking I’ll put weight back on and lose the control I’ve managed to keep.\n\nShe’s always been one for proper meals, you know—never skimps on anything. I suppose if I didn’t get that worry in my head, I’d probably manage a bit more.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would lose control if you started eating more?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s the feeling, I suppose. If I start eating more, especially when my wife’s putting butter on things or gives me one of those full plates, I get this worry that it’ll all creep back on and I won’t be able to keep a handle on it.\n\nShe means well, of course—she’s always been that way with food, very generous—but when I see it, I think, no, I’ve worked hard to keep control, and I’m afraid of losing that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like eating less helps you feel more in control of your life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose it does, in a way. Eating less makes me feel like I’m keeping a handle on things. It’s not that I sit around thinking about it all day—well, not unless it’s mealtime.\n\nLike when my wife puts butter on things, or gives me a plate piled up the way she always has—she was brought up that way, you know, “a good meal keeps you going,” her mother used to say—then I get nervous. I think if I let that go, I’ll start putting weight back on and lose the control I’ve worked hard to keep.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits are a way of maintaining control over other aspects of your life?\n\nPatient: I suppose it might be, yes… though I don’t know that I think of it in such a big way. It’s more at the table, really. If my wife puts butter on something, or gives me a plate that looks too full, I get this nervous feeling that I’ll start putting weight back on and lose the control I’ve been trying to keep.\n\nShe’s always done meals properly—big portions, bit of butter, all that. Her mother was the same, actually. But for me, when I see it, I think, no, I can’t let that slip. So yes, maybe it is about control, in my own way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your eating habits are the main way you maintain control in your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it’s the main way… I mean, at my age there aren’t so many things I’m in charge of the way I used to be. I used to have my routines, work and all that, and now it’s mostly the house, appointments, the garden when the weather’s decent.\n\nBut with food, yes, that’s where I feel it most. If my wife puts butter on something, or gives me one of those full plates—she’s only trying to look after me—I get nervous. I think, if I start eating like that, the weight will come back on, and then I’ve lost the control I’ve worked so hard to keep. So… maybe it is one of the main ways, yes.\n\nDoctor: Is your decision to eat less mostly because you are afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly, I think. It’s the weight coming back that worries me.\n\nLike if my wife puts butter on the potatoes or gives me a plate that looks too full, I get all on edge. She means well, she’s always cooked that way—proper meals, you know—but I look at it and think, if I start letting that in again, I’ll put weight back on and lose the bit of control I’ve managed to keep. So yes, that fear is a big part of why I eat less.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of gaining weight the main reason you restrict your food intake?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it probably is. I don’t like saying it that plainly, but when my wife puts butter on things, or she gives me a plate that looks too full, I get this tight, nervous feeling.\n\nIt reminds me of how she’s always done meals—proper portions, like people used to. My mother was a bit like that too, never liked waste. But I look at it now and think, if I start eating all that, the weight will come back on, and then I’ve lost the control I’ve tried to keep. So yes, the fear of gaining weight is a big part of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would look good if you gained a bit of weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t know about “look good,” exactly. My wife would probably say I’d look better with a bit more on me—she’s said things like that before, in her way.\n\nBut when I look in the mirror, if my stomach seems even a little rounded, I feel… sloppy, I suppose. Disappointed in myself. And then silly things matter, like if my belt feels a notch looser, I think, well, I’ve done right today. It’s daft, I know. I used to have a brown leather belt I wore for years, had to punch an extra hole in it once—anyway… no, I don’t really think I’d look good gaining weight, not in the way I see myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look too thin now?\n\nPatient: Too thin? I don’t really see it that way, no. I mean, I know people keep making a fuss, my wife especially, but at my age you don’t eat like you did when you were thirty, do you?\n\nI’m still getting about, doing my chores and going on my little tours when I can. I did one last week—well, not a proper tour, just out and about—but I managed fine. So I suppose I don’t quite understand why everyone’s so worried about my weight.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is currently at a healthy level for your body?\n\nPatient: I think it’s healthy enough, yes. I mean, I don’t really see why everyone’s making such a fuss. Older people don’t eat the way they did when they were younger, do they?\n\nI still manage my chores, and I still get out on my little tours when I can—well, not grand trips or anything, just my usual rounds. So from where I’m standing, if I’m still doing all that, it can’t be as bad as people make out.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the only reason you eat less than you probably should?\n\nPatient: Not the only reason, perhaps, but it’s a big part of it, if I’m being honest.\n\nWhen my wife puts butter on things—she does that, says it’ll “do me good”—or gives me a full plate, I get… unsettled. Nervous, really. Like if I let that start, I’ll just put weight back on and lose the bit of control I’ve worked quite hard to keep.\n\nIt sounds silly when I say it. She used to make lovely roast potatoes, years ago, proper crisp ones… everyone would have seconds. But now I see a plate piled up and I’m already thinking how to leave some of it without making a row.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the way it should be because of how you want to look, or because of how you want to feel?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s a bit of both, though I wouldn’t say I’m trying to look like anything in particular. Fear_of_Weight_Gain\n  description: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.\n  manifestation: When my wife tries to put butter on things or serves me a full plate, I get nervous that I'll start putting weight back on and lose the control I've worked to keep.\n- key: S046\n  name: Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance\n  description: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.\n  manifestation: If my belt feels a notch looser I feel like I've done right by myself, but if my stomach looks the least bit rounded in the mirror I feel sloppy and disappointed.\n- key: S084\n  name: Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition\n  description: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.\n  manifestation: I don't really see why everyone is making such a fuss about my weight, since older folks are supposed to eat less and I'm still managing my chores and tours.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: I still keep up with most chores, but my wife has started cutting our outings short or insisting I sit out community tours fear of gaining weight the only reason you eat less than you probably should?\n\nPatient: Not the only reason, perhaps, but it’s a big part of it, if I’m being honest.\n\nWhen my wife puts butter on things—she does that, says it’ll “do me good”—or gives me a full plate, I get… unsettled. She used to make lovely roast potatoes, years ago, proper crisp ones… everyone would have seconds. But now I see a plate piled up and I’m already thinking how to leave some of it without making a row.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the way it should be because of how you want to look, or because of how you want to feel?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s a bit of both, though I wouldn’t say I’m trying to look like anything in particular. At my age, you’re not standing in front of the mirror admiring yourself, are you?\n\nBut… if my belt feels a notch looser, I do feel like I’ve done right by myself somehow. Like I’ve kept things in order. And then if I catch myself in the mirror and my stomach looks even a little rounded, I feel… sloppy, I suppose. Disappointed with myself.\n\nFunny, I still have an old belt from years ago, good leather one, lasted better than most things we bought back then. They don’t make them like that now. But yes, I do notice where it sits. More than I probably should.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you eat less than you probably should?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’d say it’s the main one, really. I don’t like admitting that, but when my wife puts butter on things, or gives me one of those plates filled right to the edges, I get nervous.\n\nIt’s not the butter itself, you know—well, I suppose it is—but it’s more the thought that if I start eating like that again I’ll put weight back on, and then I’ll lose the bit of control I’ve managed to keep. My wife means well, of course. She’s always been one for making sure people are fed properly. Yes… partly, I think. Not wanting to look bigger, I suppose.

It’s odd what you notice. If my belt feels a notch looser, I feel as if I’ve done something right, kept myself in check. [
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, my wife, really, she’s the one who wanted me to come in. She says I’m not eating enough. I don’t know… most days I just have coffee and a piece of toast in the morning, and later maybe some soup or vegetables. I’ve never been one for big meals, even when I was younger—my father used to say dinner should “stick to your ribs,” but that always seemed like too much to me. At my age, big meals just feel unnecessary, you know?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose I have, at least that’s what my doctor keeps saying—the number on the scale has gotten too low. But I don’t know, being a bit lighter doesn’t feel like such a terrible thing to me. It makes it easier to move around the garden, and I can keep up with my walks better. I’ve always liked being out there with the tomatoes and the beans, you know, bending down and not feeling weighed down.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about gaining weight if you eat more than you currently do?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, a bit. When my wife puts butter on the toast or piles the plate up like she’s feeding a farmhand, I get nervous. Not angry, exactly, just uneasy, like I’ll start putting weight back on and lose the control I’ve kept.\n\nShe means well, of course. She’s always been a good cook—used to make these big Sunday dinners when the children were around—but now it just feels like too much on the plate. So yes, I do worry about it some.\n\nDoctor: How often do you think about gaining weight during the day?\n\nPatient: Not all day, no… at least I don’t think so. It’s more when food is right in front of me. Like if my wife puts butter on the toast or gives me a plate that looks too full, then I start thinking, “Oh, here we go, I’ll put the weight back on.” It makes me nervous, like I’ll lose the control I’ve been trying to keep.\n\nFunny, she’s always believed a proper meal fixes everything—her mother was the same way, big plates, gravy on everything—but for me it just sort of sits in my mind when she serves it that way.\n\nDoctor: Is the worry about gaining weight something that you think about even when you're not eating or thinking about food?\n\nPatient: Not so much when I’m out in the garden or reading the paper, no. It’s mostly when food’s there in front of me—like if my wife tries to put butter on things, or serves me one of those full plates. Then I get nervous I’ll start putting weight back on and lose the control I’ve been keeping.\n\nShe’s always been generous with food, bless her… never could make a small portion of anything. But yes, it’s more around meals than just popping into my head all day.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat more if you weren't worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I might, yes… if that nervous feeling wasn’t there. It’s usually when my wife’s putting butter on something or gives me a plate that looks too full, then I start thinking I’ll put weight back on and lose the control I’ve managed to keep.\n\nShe’s always been one for proper meals, you know—never skimps on anything. I suppose if I didn’t get that worry in my head, I’d probably manage a bit more.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would lose control if you started eating more?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s the feeling, I suppose. If I start eating more, especially when my wife’s putting butter on things or gives me one of those full plates, I get this worry that it’ll all creep back on and I won’t be able to keep a handle on it.\n\nShe means well, of course—she’s always been that way with food, very generous—but when I see it, I think, no, I’ve worked hard to keep control, and I’m afraid of losing that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like eating less helps you feel more in control of your life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose it does, in a way. Eating less makes me feel like I’m keeping a handle on things. It’s not that I sit around thinking about it all day—well, not unless it’s mealtime.\n\nLike when my wife puts butter on things, or gives me a plate piled up the way she always has—she was brought up that way, you know, “a good meal keeps you going,” her mother used to say—then I get nervous. I think if I let that go, I’ll start putting weight back on and lose the control I’ve worked hard to keep.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits are a way of maintaining control over other aspects of your life?\n\nPatient: I suppose it might be, yes… though I don’t know that I think of it in such a big way. It’s more at the table, really. If my wife puts butter on something, or gives me a plate that looks too full, I get this nervous feeling that I’ll start putting weight back on and lose the control I’ve been trying to keep.\n\nShe’s always done meals properly—big portions, bit of butter, all that. Her mother was the same, actually. But for me, when I see it, I think, no, I can’t let that slip. So yes, maybe it is about control, in my own way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your eating habits are the main way you maintain control in your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it’s the main way… I mean, at my age there aren’t so many things I’m in charge of the way I used to be. I used to have my routines, work and all that, and now it’s mostly the house, appointments, the garden when the weather’s decent.\n\nBut with food, yes, that’s where I feel it most. If my wife puts butter on something, or gives me one of those full plates—she’s only trying to look after me—I get nervous. I think, if I start eating like that, the weight will come back on, and then I’ve lost the control I’ve worked so hard to keep. So… maybe it is one of the main ways, yes.\n\nDoctor: Is your decision to eat less mostly because you are afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly, I think. It’s the weight coming back that worries me.\n\nLike if my wife puts butter on the potatoes or gives me a plate that looks too full, I get all on edge. She means well, she’s always cooked that way—proper meals, you know—but I look at it and think, if I start letting that in again, I’ll put weight back on and lose the bit of control I’ve managed to keep. So yes, that fear is a big part of why I eat less.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of gaining weight the main reason you restrict your food intake?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it probably is. I don’t like saying it that plainly, but when my wife puts butter on things, or she gives me a plate that looks too full, I get this tight, nervous feeling.\n\nIt reminds me of how she’s always done meals—proper portions, like people used to. My mother was a bit like that too, never liked waste. But I look at it now and think, if I start eating all that, the weight will come back on, and then I’ve lost the control I’ve tried to keep. So yes, the fear of gaining weight is a big part of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would look good if you gained a bit of weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t know about “look good,” exactly. My wife would probably say I’d look better with a bit more on me—she’s said things like that before, in her way.\n\nBut when I look in the mirror, if my stomach seems even a little rounded, I feel… sloppy, I suppose. Disappointed in myself. And then silly things matter, like if my belt feels a notch looser, I think, well, I’ve done right today. It’s daft, I know. I used to have a brown leather belt I wore for years, had to punch an extra hole in it once—anyway… no, I don’t really think I’d look good gaining weight, not in the way I see myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look too thin now?\n\nPatient: Too thin? I don’t really see it that way, no. I mean, I know people keep making a fuss, my wife especially, but at my age you don’t eat like you did when you were thirty, do you?\n\nI’m still getting about, doing my chores and going on my little tours when I can. I did one last week—well, not a proper tour, just out and about—but I managed fine. So I suppose I don’t quite understand why everyone’s so worried about my weight.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is currently at a healthy level for your body?\n\nPatient: I think it’s healthy enough, yes. I mean, I don’t really see why everyone’s making such a fuss. Older people don’t eat the way they did when they were younger, do they?\n\nI still manage my chores, and I still get out on my little tours when I can—well, not grand trips or anything, just my usual rounds. So from where I’m standing, if I’m still doing all that, it can’t be as bad as people make out.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the only reason you eat less than you probably should?\n\nPatient: Not the only reason, perhaps, but it’s a big part of it, if I’m being honest.\n\nWhen my wife puts butter on things—she does that, says it’ll “do me good”—or gives me a full plate, I get… unsettled. Nervous, really. Like if I let that start, I’ll just put weight back on and lose the bit of control I’ve worked quite hard to keep.\n\nIt sounds silly when I say it. She used to make lovely roast potatoes, years ago, proper crisp ones… everyone would have seconds. But now I see a plate piled up and I’m already thinking how to leave some of it without making a row.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the way it should be because of how you want to look, or because of how you want to feel?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s a bit of both, though I wouldn’t say I’m trying to look like anything in particular. At my age, you’re not standing in front of the mirror admiring yourself, are you?\n\nBut… if my belt feels a notch looser, I do feel like I’ve done right by myself somehow. Like I’ve kept things in order. And then if I catch myself in the mirror and my stomach looks even a little rounded, I feel… sloppy, I suppose. Disappointed with myself.\n\nFunny, I still have an old belt from years ago, good leather one, lasted better than most things we bought back then. They don’t make them like that now. But yes, I do notice where it sits. More than I probably should.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you eat less than you probably should?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’d say it’s the main one, really. I don’t like admitting that, but when my wife puts butter on things, or gives me one of those plates filled right to the edges, I get nervous.\n\nIt’s not the butter itself, you know—well, I suppose it is—but it’s more the thought that if I start eating like that again I’ll put weight back on, and then I’ll lose the bit of control I’ve managed to keep. My wife means well, of course. She’s always been one for making sure people are fed properly. But yes, that fear is a big part of why I hold back.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because of how you want to look?\n\nPatient: Yes… partly, I think. Not wanting to look bigger, I suppose.\n\nIt’s odd what you notice. If my belt feels a notch looser, I feel as if I’ve done something right, kept myself in check. I’ve had that belt ages, actually—proper leather, not like the flimsy things they sell now. But then if I see my stomach in the mirror and it looks even a little rounded, I feel… sloppy. Disappointed in myself, really. So yes, I suppose how I look does come into it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

If my wife puts butter on something, or gives me one of those full plates—she’s only trying to look after me—I get nervous. I think, if I start eating like that, the weight will come back on, and then I’ve lost the control I’ve worked so hard to keep. So… maybe it is one of the main ways, yes.\n\nDoctor: Is your decision to eat less mostly because you are afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly, I think. It’s the weight coming back that worries me.\n\nLike if my wife puts butter on the potatoes or gives me a plate that looks too full, I get all on edge. She means well, she’s always cooked that way—proper meals, you know—but I look at it and think, if I start letting that in again, I’ll put weight back on and lose the bit of control I’ve managed to keep. So yes, that fear is a big part of why I eat less.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of gaining weight the main reason you restrict your food intake?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it probably is. I don’t like saying it that plainly, but when my wife puts butter on things, or she gives me a plate that looks too full, I get this tight, nervous feeling.\n\nIt reminds me of how she’s always done meals—proper portions, like people used to. My mother was a bit like that too, never liked waste. But I look at it now and think, if I start eating all that, the weight will come back on, and then I’ve lost the control I’ve tried to keep. So yes, the fear of gaining weight is a big part of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would look good if you gained a bit of weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t know about “look good,” exactly. My wife would probably say I’d look better with a bit more on me—she’s said things like that before, in her way.\n\nBut when I look in the mirror, if my stomach seems even a little rounded, I feel… sloppy, I suppose. Disappointed in myself. And then silly things matter, like if my belt feels a notch looser, I think, well, I’ve done right today. It’s daft, I know. I used to have a brown leather belt I wore for years, had to punch an extra hole in it once—anyway… no, I don’t really think I’d look good gaining weight, not in the way I see myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look too thin now?\n\nPatient: Too thin? I don’t really see it that way, no. I mean, I know people keep making a fuss, my wife especially, but at my age you don’t eat like you did when you were thirty, do you?\n\nI’m still getting about, doing my chores and going on my little tours when I can. I did one last week—well, not a proper tour, just out and about—but I managed fine. So I suppose I don’t quite understand why everyone’s so worried about my weight.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is currently at a healthy level for your body?\n\nPatient: I think it’s healthy enough, yes. I mean, I don’t really see why everyone’s making such a fuss. Older people don’t eat the way they did when they were younger, do they?\n\nI still manage my chores, and I still get out on my little tours when I can—well, not grand trips or anything, just my usual rounds. So from where I’m standing, if I’m still doing all that, it can’t be as bad as people make out.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the only reason you eat less than you probably should?\n\nPatient: Not the only reason, perhaps, but it’s a big part of it, if I’m being honest.\n\nWhen my wife puts butter on things—she does that, says it’ll “do me good”—or gives me a full plate, I get… unsettled. Nervous, really. Like if I let that start, I’ll just put weight back on and lose the bit of control I’ve worked quite hard to keep.\n\nIt sounds silly when I say it. She used to make lovely roast potatoes, years ago, proper crisp ones… everyone would have seconds. But now I see a plate piled up and I’m already thinking how to leave some of it without making a row.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the way it should be because of how you want to look, or because of how you want to feel?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s a bit of both, though I wouldn’t say I’m trying to look like anything in particular. At my age, you’re not standing in front of the mirror admiring yourself, are you?\n\nBut… if my belt feels a notch looser, I do feel like I’ve done right by myself somehow. Like I’ve kept things in order. And then if I catch myself in the mirror and my stomach looks even a little rounded, I feel… sloppy, I suppose. Disappointed with myself.\n\nFunny, I still have an old belt from years ago, good leather one, lasted better than most things we bought back then. They don’t make them like that now. But yes, I do notice where it sits. More than I probably should.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you eat less than you probably should?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’d say it’s the main one, really. I don’t like admitting that, but when my wife puts butter on things, or gives me one of those plates filled right to the edges, I get nervous.\n\nIt’s not the butter itself, you know—well, I suppose it is—but it’s more the thought that if I start eating like that again I’ll put weight back on, and then I’ll lose the bit of control I’ve managed to keep. My wife means well, of course. She’s always been one for making sure people are fed properly. But yes, that fear is a big part of why I hold back.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because of how you want to look?\n\nPatient: Yes… partly, I think. Not wanting to look bigger, I suppose.\n\nIt’s odd what you notice. If my belt feels a notch looser, I feel as if I’ve done something right, kept myself in check. I’ve had that belt ages, actually—proper leather, not like the flimsy things they sell now. But then if I see my stomach in the mirror and it looks even a little rounded, I feel… sloppy. Disappointed in myself, really. So yes, I suppose how I look does come into it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Not the only thing, no… I mean, that’s part of it, but I suppose what brings me in is my wife thinking I’m not eating enough.

Most mornings I’ll just have coffee and a bit of toast, and then later on maybe some soup, or vegetables if there are any made. Big meals just feel unnecessary at my age, you know? When you’re younger you’re out working, burning it off, needing a proper plateful. I remember taking sandwiches to work, wrapped in wax paper—my wife used to do them just so.

Now it just feels like too much fuss, too much food. So yes, the weight worries me, but I also tell myself I simply don’t need as much as I used to.

More than I probably should.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you eat less than you probably should?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’d say it’s the main one, really. I don’t like admitting that, but when my wife puts butter on things, or gives me one of those plates filled right to the edges, I get nervous.\n\nIt’s not the butter itself, you know—well, I suppose it is—but it’s more the thought that if I start eating like that again I’ll put weight back on, and then I’ll lose the bit of control I’ve managed to keep. My wife means well, of course. She’s always been one for making sure people are fed properly. But yes, that fear is a big part of why I hold back.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because of how you want to look?\n\nPatient: Yes… partly, I think. Not wanting to look bigger, I suppose.\n\nIt’s odd what you notice. If my belt feels a notch looser, I feel as if I’ve done something right, kept myself in check. Disappointed in myself, really.
